=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
Tiles are laid out covering the two-dimensional coordinate plane.
Each tile is a rectangle, and for each integer triple (i, j, k) satisfying 0 \leq k < K, a corresponding tile is placed according to the following rules:

- When i and j have the same parity (both even or both odd), the tile corresponding to (i, j, k) covers the area where iK \leq x \leq (i + 1)K and jK + k \leq y \leq jK + k + 1.
- When i and j have different parity, the tile corresponding to (i, j, k) covers the area where iK + k \leq x \leq iK + k + 1 and jK \leq y \leq (j + 1)K.

Two tiles are adjacent when their edges have a common segment of positive length.
Starting from the tile containing the point (S_x + 0.5, S_y + 0.5), find the minimum number of times you need to move to an adjacent tile to reach the tile containing the point (T_x + 0.5, T_y + 0.5).
There are T test cases; solve each of them.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
T
\text{case}_1
\vdots
\text{case}_T

Each case is given in the following format:
K S_x S_y T_x T_y

Output

Print T lines. The i-th line should contain the answer for the i-th test case.

Constraints


- 1 \leq T \leq 10^4
- 2 \leq K \leq 10^{16}
- -10^{16} \leq S_x, S_y, T_x, T_y \leq 10^{16}
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

3
3 -2 1 4 -1
4 8 8 0 2
5 -1000000000000 -1000000000000 1000000000000 1000000000000

Sample Output 1

4
4
800000000000

Let us explain the first test case.
Let (i, j, k) denote the tile corresponding to integer triple (i, j, k).
(-1.5, 1.5) is contained in tile (-1, 0, 1), and (4.5, -0.5) is contained in tile (1, -1, 2).
For example, by moving from tile (-1, 0, 1) to (-1, 0, 2) to (0, 0, 2) to (1, 0, 0) to (1, -1, 2), you can reach tile (1, -1, 2) in four moves to an adjacent tile.

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's explore graph structure.

Observation: The tiling is periodic with period? K? But orientation pattern checkerboard of blocks. Within each block K slices. Adjacent across block boundaries often connects many-to-one/all-to-all. This may allow shortcuts: to move from one block to neighbor, cost 1 if you are at appropriate slice(s). But you can change slice within block at cost |delta k|. Across certain boundaries, you can change k arbitrarily? For example H-block to V-block across vertical side: H_k to V_boundary for all k, cost 1. Thus from any H_k you can enter V_boundary. Then inside V-block, to reach another V_k cost |k - boundary|. So crossing vertical side from H to V costs 1 + distance within V to desired k. But maybe if you only need enter V block, you land at boundary slice (0 or K-1). Similarly H to V across horizontal side: only H_0/H_{K-1} can cross to any V_k cost 1, so if you are not boundary H slice, need move vertically inside H to boundary first, then cross.

Need derive distance formula.

Let's define block coordinates (i,j). The orientation parity p = (i+j) mod 2? H if i,j same parity => i+j even. V if i+j odd. Let's call H when (i+j) even, V when odd. Adjacent block flips parity.

Within block, slice index k (0..K-1). For H, k is y-offset b; for V, k is x-offset a.

Movement inside block: H: k +/-1 cost1; V: k +/-1 cost1. So internal coordinate can change by 1 per move. Across boundaries, there are transitions that can change k in certain ways.

Let's enumerate directed/undirected edge types between H and V blocks:

Let H block A at (i,j), V block B at neighbor.

Case B to the right of A: B=(i+1,j). A's right side: all H_k adjacent to B's V_0. So transition H_k <-> V_0 for any k. Thus from V_0 to any H_k cost1; from any H_k to V_0 cost1. This boundary is "V_0 hub" connected to all H_k. We'll call type R (right neighbor): H -- V_0.

Case B to the left of A: B=(i-1,j). A's left side: all H_k adjacent to B's V_{K-1}. So H_k <-> V_{K-1}. Type L: H -- V_{K-1}.

Case B above A: B=(i,j+1). A's top side: only H_{K-1} adjacent to all V_k. So H_{K-1} <-> V_k for all k. Type U: H_{K-1} -- V_all.
Case B below A: B=(i,j-1). A's bottom side: only H_0 adjacent to all V_k. H_0 <-> V_all. Type D: H_0 -- V_all.

From perspective V block:
- V_0 connects to all H_k of right neighbor? Wait if V block at (i,j), H right (i+1,j): V_0 right edge adjacent to all H_k. So V_0 -- H_all. That's same as H left? For H block left of V, H right side: H_k -- V_0. Yes.
- V_{K-1} connects to all H_k of left neighbor.
- V_k all connect to H_0 of above neighbor? If V at (i,j), H above (i,j+1): V_k top edge adjacent to H_0 (bottom slice of above). So V_all -- H_0.
- V_k all connect to H_{K-1} of below neighbor.

Thus each boundary is either:
- A "side" boundary (vertical between H and V): connects a single V boundary slice (0 or K-1) to all H slices.
- A "top/bottom" boundary (horizontal): connects a single H boundary slice (0 or K-1) to all V slices.

So each block has four sides:
H block:
  left: V_{K-1} hub connected to all H_k.
  right: V_0 hub connected to all H_k.
  bottom: H_0 connected to all V_k (in V below).
  top: H_{K-1} connected to all V_k (in V above).
V block:
  left: V_0 connected to all H_k (in H left).
  right: V_{K-1} connected to all H_k (in H right).
  bottom: all V_k connected to H_{K-1} (in H below).
  top: all V_k connected to H_0 (in H above).

This graph is bipartite between H and V blocks (since block parity alternates). Edges only between H and V.

- H block: k along y (vertical). Ports to left/right neighbors are at any k (all H_k) but connect to V boundary k=0/K-1. Ports to bottom/top are at k=0/K-1 but connect to all V_k.
- V block: k along x (horizontal). Ports to bottom/top are at any k (all V_k) but connect to H boundary k=0/K-1. Ports to left/right are at k=0/K-1 but connect to all H_k.

So each block has four sides. For each side, the "interface" is either:
- all k on this block connect to a single k on neighbor (call A-side), or
- single k on this block connects to all k on neighbor (call B-side).

Specifically:
H block:
  left/right: A-side? all H_k to V_{K-1}/V_0 (single neighbor k). So from H, any k can cross to neighbor with k fixed (0 or K-1). This is "exit with any k, enter neighbor at port".
  bottom/top: B-side? single H_0/H_{K-1} to all V_k. From H, only boundary k can cross, but can enter neighbor at any k.
V block:
  bottom/top: A-side? all V_k to H_{K-1}/H_0 (single neighbor k). From V, any k can cross to neighbor with k fixed.
  left/right: B-side? single V_0/V_{K-1} to all H_k. From V, only boundary k can cross, but enter neighbor at any k.

Thus the type of side depends on orientation of block and direction:
- For H block, vertical sides are "all-to-one" (A), horizontal sides are "one-to-all" (B).
- For V block, horizontal sides are "all-to-one" (A), vertical sides are "one-to-all" (B).

So in any block, the sides parallel to the long dimension? H long in x; vertical sides (x boundaries) are all-to-one. V long in y; horizontal sides are all-to-one. The sides perpendicular to long dimension are one-to-all.

This is like each block has two "highways" directions.

Consider the "ports" at k=0 and k=K-1. The all-to-one sides allow crossing from any k to a port (0 or K-1) in neighbor. The one-to-all sides allow crossing from a port in current block to any k in neighbor. Therefore, to move between blocks, the k value after crossing is either:
- If crossing an A-side from current block: current k arbitrary, neighbor k fixed to 0 or K-1.
- If crossing a B-side from current block: current k must be fixed (0 or K-1), neighbor k arbitrary.

Thus the "free" k changes only when crossing B-side from a port to arbitrary, or when crossing A-side from arbitrary to port. So k can be reset to arbitrary at cost maybe by going through a port.

But is block Manhattan distance always the base? The block graph (ignoring k) is a square grid: each block has four neighboring blocks. Let's characterize when a path of length D = |Δi|+|Δj| exists. Along such a path, each move must cross to a neighboring block that reduces Manhattan distance to target (no staying in same block, no backtracking). Thus the sequence of block moves is a monotone path in the grid from start block to end block: at each step move in one of the two coordinate directions that gets closer (if Δi,Δj signs). There are C(D, |Δi|) possible monotone paths. Need see if there exists a monotone path such that the required k at each block is compatible with start/end and crossing rules, possibly with no extra k-change moves. Since each block move costs 1 and no internal moves. At each block, the k before crossing must satisfy the side's requirement (for B-side) or can be arbitrary (for A-side), and after crossing the k in next block is determined (for A-side) or arbitrary (for B-side). Let's formalize.

We have a path of blocks B_0, B_1, ..., B_D, where B_{m+1} is adjacent to B_m. For each move m from block B_m to B_{m+1}, there is a side of B_m and a side of B_{m+1}. The crossing imposes a relation between k_m (slice in B_m) and k_{m+1} (slice in B_{m+1}). We need choose k_0 = start k, k_D = end k, and intermediate k values such that for each move relation holds. If such exists, distance = D. If not, need extra moves.

Let's derive relation for each directed move between H and V.

Let's define block orientation O(i,j) = H if i+j even, V if odd.

Move directions: E (i+1,j), W (i-1,j), N (j+1), S (j-1).

We can express relation R_{O, dir}(k_current, k_next).

From earlier:

Case current H, move E to V (right):
- H right side all H_k to V_0. Relation: k_next = 0. k_current arbitrary.
Current H, move W to V (left):
- H left side all H_k to V_{K-1}. Relation: k_next = K-1.
Current H, move N to V (above):
- H top side H_{K-1} to all V_k. Relation: k_current = K-1. k_next arbitrary.
Current H, move S to V (below):
- H bottom side H_0 to all V_k. Relation: k_current = 0. k_next arbitrary.

Current V, move E to H (right):
- V right side V_{K-1} to all H_k. Relation: k_current = K-1. k_next arbitrary.
Current V, move W to H (left):
- V left side V_0 to all H_k. Relation: k_current = 0. k_next arbitrary.
Current V, move N to H (above):
- V top side all V_k to H_0. Relation: k_next = 0. k_current arbitrary.
Current V, move S to H (below):
- V bottom side all V_k to H_{K-1}. Relation: k_next = K-1. k_current arbitrary.

Notice relation always fixes either current k or next k to a boundary (0 or K-1), never both, and the other is free. Specifically:
- If move is along the "long" direction? Let's summarize:
  * E from H fixes next=0; W from H fixes next=K-1. (H to V horizontal moves fix next k)
  * N from H fixes current=K-1; S from H fixes current=0. (H to V vertical moves fix current k)
  * E from V fixes current=K-1; W from V fixes current=0. (V to H horizontal moves fix current k)
  * N from V fixes next=0; S from V fixes next=K-1. (V to H vertical moves fix next k)

So each move has a "fixed" endpoint: either the k in the block being exited (current) or the k in the block being entered (next), equal to 0 or K-1. The other endpoint is free. Thus along a monotone path, constraints are on k at certain blocks depending on the direction used to enter/exit that block. For each intermediate block, it is entered from previous block (one move) and exited to next block (next move). The k at that block may be constrained by the entry move (if entry fixes next) or by the exit move (if exit fixes current), or both, or neither? Need check. If both constraints exist, they must be compatible (same boundary value), otherwise no zero-extra path through that block. If one or none, can choose k accordingly (but also k must be integer 0..K-1; boundary values valid).

Start block k is fixed to start_k. It is only constrained by first exit move if that move fixes current; if first move fixes next, start k free (but fixed by start). End block k fixed to end_k; constrained by last entry move if entry fixes next; if last move fixes current, end k free.

Thus zero-extra path existence reduces to checking constraints along a monotone path. Since there may be many monotone paths, need determine if any path satisfies.

Let's express constraints in terms of block orientation and direction.

Maybe the fixed boundary value (0 or K-1) depends on direction and orientation. Let's encode k values as 0 and 1 for "low" and "high" boundary. Let b = K-1.

For a move from block A to adjacent B:
- If A is H:
  - E: next k = 0.
  - W: next k = b.
  - N: current k = b.
  - S: current k = 0.
- If A is V:
  - E: current k = b.
  - W: current k = 0.
  - N: next k = 0.
  - S: next k = b.

Can we simplify using parity and direction? Let's compute orientation of neighbor:
If A=H (i+j even):
  E: B=(i+1,j), i+j+1 odd => V. Fix next=0.

Let's define for each block along a monotone path:
- arrival constraint A: if the move entering this block fixes next k, then arrival k is fixed to value a (0 or b). Otherwise arrival k is free (can be chosen).
- departure constraint D: if the move leaving this block fixes current k, then departure k must be fixed to value d (0 or b). Otherwise departure k is free.

At start block, arrival k is start_k (fixed by start tile). At end block, departure k is end_k (fixed by end tile). Actually end block has no departure; final k must equal end_k.

  Need adjust from a to d if a != d. Cost = adj(a,d) = min(|a-d|,2) (0 if same, 1 if K=2? a,d are 0/b; if K=2, |0-1|=1; if K>2, |0-b|=K-1, min=2). If arrival fixed, departure free: no adjustment needed; departure k can be whatever arrival k is, and exit side that leaves k free accepts any k. So cost 0.
If arrival free, departure fixed: we can choose arrival k (via previous move's free endpoint) to equal d, no cost. If arrival free, departure free: no cost.

Start block: arrival k is start_k (fixed), not free. End block: departure k is end_k (fixed), not free.

We can express the fixed endpoint and value in terms of the coordinate of the fixed block and the direction.

Maybe define for any block C and a move incident to C:
- If the move is "vertical" (N/S) and C is H? Let's table by block C and side through which it is entered/exited.

  If C=H:

  If C=H:

    If C=H:

Let's tabulate for block C orientation:

H block:
- Arrival fixed when entered from bottom/top (vertical sides): value low if from bottom, high if from top.
- Arrival free when entered from left/right (horizontal sides).
- Departure fixed when exited to bottom/top: value low if S, high if N.
- Departure free when exited to left/right.

V block:
- Arrival fixed when entered from left/right: value low if from left, high if from right.
- Arrival free when entered from bottom/top.
- Departure fixed when exited to left/right: value low if W, high if E.
- Departure free when exited to bottom/top.

This is symmetric: For H, constraints occur on vertical movement (N/S); for V, constraints occur on horizontal movement (E/W). The fixed value is low for movement in negative direction? Let's see:
H: entered from bottom (S) -> low; exited to bottom (S) -> low. Entered from top (N) -> high; exited to top (N) -> high. So for H, vertical movement: fixed value = low if moving downward (decreasing y), high if moving upward (increasing y). Both arrival and departure.
V: entered from left (W) -> low; exited to left (W) -> low. Entered from right (E) -> high; exited to right (E) -> high. So for V, horizontal movement: fixed value = low if moving left (decreasing x), high if moving right (increasing x).

Thus constraint value depends on the direction of the move along the axis that is "stacked" for that block:
- H block's stacked axis is y. Moving in y direction (N/S) fixes k to low for S, high for N.
- V block's stacked axis is x. Moving in x direction (E/W) fixes k to low for W, high for E.

Moves along the other axis leave k free.

This is much simpler! Let's verify:
H block:
- Move E/W (x direction): free departure; arrival from E/W free. Yes.
- Move N/S (y direction): fixed departure/arrival with value high for N, low for S.
V block:
- Move E/W (x direction): fixed departure/arrival with value high for E, low for W.
- Move N/S (y direction): free.

So the fixed boundary value equals:
- If the move is along the block's "stacked" axis, the sign of the move: + -> high, - -> low.
- If along the other axis, no constraint.

This is elegant.

Now, along a monotone path from start block to end block, we have a sequence of moves E/W/N/S. For each block, the incoming and outgoing moves may be along its stacked axis or not. The block orientation H/V is determined by parity of (i+j). But note the stacked axis:
- H (i+j even): stacked axis y.
- V (i+j odd): stacked axis x.

Thus for a block, whether a move direction is along its stacked axis depends on orientation:
  H: N/S are constrained; E/W free.
  V: E/W constrained; N/S free.

The constrained value for a move:
  If constrained and direction is + (E for V, N for H) -> high (b).
  If constrained and direction is - (W for V, S for H) -> low (0).

Now, for a block along path, arrival constraint exists if the incoming move is along the block's stacked axis. Departure constraint exists if the outgoing move is along the block's stacked axis. If both exist, we need compare values: arrival value from incoming direction, departure value from outgoing direction. If they differ, adjustment cost.

Let's compute values in terms of move directions:
For H block:
  incoming from S (move N into H): arrival low.
  incoming from N (move S into H): arrival high.
  outgoing to S (move S out): departure low.
  outgoing to N (move N out): departure high.
For V block:
  incoming from W (move E into V): arrival low.
  incoming from E (move W into V): arrival high.
  outgoing to W (move W out): departure low.
  outgoing to E (move E out): departure high.

So the value is low if the move direction (from previous block to current, or current to next) is along negative axis in the block's stacked coordinate: for H, negative y = S; for V, negative x = W. High if positive axis: H N, V E.

Now, along a monotone path, the sequence of moves consists of some horizontal E/W and vertical N/S. The block orientation alternates? Actually adjacent blocks have opposite parity, so orientation alternates with every move. But the stacked axis of a block is not simply alternating? H/V alternate along any path, yes every step flips parity, so orientation alternates H,V,H,V... Therefore along a path, the stacked axis alternates between y and x each block. So if at block m orientation O_m, next block O_{m+1} = opposite.

This may allow simplification: The constrained moves are those where the move direction matches the stacked axis of the block from which/into which? Let's see.

For a move from block A to B:
- If A is H, move N/S constrains A (departure); move E/W free.
- If B is H, incoming move N/S constrains B (arrival); move E/W free.

Every horizontal move (E/W) constrains the V endpoint (departure if V source, arrival if V target). Nice! Because H stacked axis y, V stacked axis x. So:
- Vertical moves (N/S) always impose a constraint on the H block involved in the move (source if H->V, target if V->H).
- Horizontal moves (E/W) always impose a constraint on the V block involved in the move (source if V->H, target if H->V).

The constraint value:
For a vertical move:
  If moving N (+y): H block's k must be high (b).
  If moving S (-y): H block's k must be low (0).
For a horizontal move:
  If moving E (+x): V block's k must be high (b).
  If moving W (-x): V block's k must be low (0).

- For H block: bottom side = low, top side = high.

Let's rephrase:
A move between adjacent blocks crosses a side of each block. For the block that is constrained by the move:
- If the constrained block is H, the side crossed is either bottom or top; value low for bottom, high for top.
- If the constrained block is V, the side crossed is either left or right; value low for left, high for right.
The unconstrained block is the one for which the crossed side is along its long dimension? H left/right free; V bottom/top free.

Thus along a path, each move constrains exactly one of the two blocks:
- If the move is vertical (N/S), the H block (which is either source or target) is constrained at its bottom/top side.
- If the move is horizontal (E/W), the V block is constrained at its left/right side.

The constrained value is low if the side is "negative" (bottom for H, left for V), high if "positive" (top for H, right for V).

Now, for a block along a path, it may be constrained by the incoming move (if that move crosses a constrained side of this block) and/or by the outgoing move. It cannot be constrained by both? Let's check: A block has two incident moves along the path, one incoming and one outgoing. Could both constrain the same block? Yes if the path passes through a block and both moves cross constrained sides of that block. For an H block, constrained sides are bottom/top. If path enters/exits H via vertical moves (N/S) both, then both constrain H. But can a monotone path enter and exit an H block via vertical moves? That would mean two consecutive vertical moves in same direction? Example path S then S through H? Blocks: V above -> H -> V below. Incoming move S? Let's define. If path goes from V above to H (move S into H? Actually V at (i,j+1) to H at (i,j) is move S from V to H, crosses H top side, constrains H high). Then from H to V below is move S from H to V, crosses H bottom side, constrains H low. So H has arrival high and departure low, both constrained. This occurs when path moves vertically downward through an H block. Similarly upward through H: arrival low, departure high. For V block, if path moves horizontally through V (e.g., H left -> V -> H right), both incoming and outgoing horizontal moves constrain V: arrival from left low, departure to right high (if E through V), or arrival from right high, departure to left low (if W through V). So a block can be constrained twice when the path goes straight through it along its constrained axis (H vertical, V horizontal). If path turns at a block, one move may constrain it and the other may be free, or neither? Let's see:
- H block: constrained moves are vertical. If one incident move vertical and the other horizontal, H constrained once (by vertical move), horizontal free. If both horizontal, H not constrained at all (path passes horizontally through H, which is along its long direction; free).
- V block: constrained moves are horizontal. If one horizontal and one vertical, V constrained once; if both vertical, V not constrained.

Thus along a monotone path, the path is composed of horizontal and vertical segments. Blocks where the path goes straight in the constrained direction (vertical through H, horizontal through V) have two constraints (arrival and departure) with values depending on entry/exit sides. Blocks where path turns have one constraint (from the move along constrained axis) or none. Start/end have one fixed tile k plus possibly one constraint from the incident move if it is along constrained axis.

This suggests we can compute extra cost by looking at the sequence of moves and the constrained values at each block. Maybe there is an invariant: The constrained value at a block equals the coordinate of the block along the constrained axis? Let's examine.

For H block at (i,j), bottom side low, top side high. If path enters from bottom (move N) and exits to top (move N), constraints low then high. This corresponds to moving upward through H; the k must change from low to high while inside H, cost adj(low,high). If moving downward through H, high then low. If path enters from bottom and exits to left/right (turn), only arrival low; departure free, so no change needed. If path enters from left/right and exits to top, only departure high; arrival free, can choose arrival high, no cost. If path enters from left and exits to right (horizontal through H), no constraints; k can be anything, no cost. So the only cost occurs when the path goes straight through a block along its constrained axis, because then k must be low on one side and high on the other (or vice versa), requiring an adjustment. The cost for such a straight constrained pass is adj(low,high) = 1 if K=2 else 2. Wait if moving upward through H: enter bottom low, exit top high. Need change k from low to high. If K>2, cost 2 via neighbor detour? But can we change k inside H from 0 to b in 2 using a side neighbor? Yes, but if we are in the middle of a monotone path, can we detour to a side neighbor and back without increasing block coordinate? That would add 2 moves and return to same H block, then exit top. This is allowed; it doesn't change block path but adds extra. Alternatively direct internal move cost K-1, but min is 2. So cost 2. For K=2, cost 1. Thus each straight constrained pass costs c = 1 if K=2 else 2.

But wait, if the path goes straight through H vertically, could we avoid the adjustment by choosing a different monotone path that doesn't go straight through H? Yes, extra cost depends on number of such straight constrained passes in the chosen path. We can choose path to minimize.

Similarly, straight horizontal pass through V (H left -> V -> H right) requires V k low at left entry, high at right exit, cost adj(low,high). Straight horizontal pass in opposite direction requires high then low. So each straight pass through a V block horizontally costs c.

What about start/end? Start block has fixed k. If the first move constrains start (i.e., first move is along start's constrained axis), then we may need adjust from start_k to the required side value. If first move is free (along start's unconstrained axis), no cost. Similarly end: if last move constrains end, adjust from required side value to end_k; if free, no cost. Also if start/end block is passed? Start/end are endpoints, no straight pass.

What about a block where both incident moves constrain it but not straight? Is that possible? For H, both incident moves vertical would mean path enters/exits via top/bottom; that is straight vertical through H (could be same direction? In a monotone path, if both moves vertical, they must be same sign? Actually to go from V above to H to V below, incoming move S (from V above to H) and outgoing move S (from H to V below) both vertical downward. That's straight vertical. If incoming N and outgoing N would be upward. Can't have incoming N and outgoing S because that would backtrack (enter from bottom, exit bottom) not monotone. So both vertical implies straight. For V, both horizontal implies straight. Thus cost only straight passes.

Therefore, the extra cost over block Manhattan distance seems to be:
- For each time the monotone path goes straight through a block along its constrained axis (i.e., two consecutive moves in the same direction along that axis? Actually vertical through H or horizontal through V), add c.
- Plus possible endpoint adjustments if start/end k not matching the required side value for the first/last move when that move is along constrained axis.

But is that sufficient? What about a block where arrival fixed and departure free but arrival value differs from some desired departure? Since departure free, no constraint; we can exit with whatever k we have. No cost. If arrival free and departure fixed, we can choose arrival k to match departure; no cost. Good.

Now, can a monotone path have consecutive moves in the same direction? Yes if Δi or Δj magnitude >1. For example two E moves in a row. Let's examine two E moves: blocks H -> V -> H (since orientation alternates). The middle block is V, and both moves are horizontal E, so it's a straight horizontal pass through V: entry from left low, exit to right high, cost c. So every pair of consecutive horizontal moves in the same direction costs c. What about two consecutive horizontal moves but opposite directions? Not monotone (would backtrack). So in a monotone path, horizontal moves are all E if Δi>0, all W if Δi<0. Thus any time there are at least two horizontal moves, the intermediate V blocks between them incur cost c for each adjacent pair? Let's see sequence of horizontal moves of length Hcnt = |Δi|. They occur possibly interleaved with vertical moves. A straight horizontal pass through V occurs when two horizontal moves are consecutive in the path (no vertical move between), because the block between them is V and both moves horizontal. If there is a vertical move between horizontal moves, the path turns at an H block? Example E, S, E: blocks H->V (E), V->H (S), H->V (E). The middle H block has incoming horizontal (free) and outgoing vertical (constrained), so no straight pass. The V blocks at ends have one horizontal move each, not two. So no straight horizontal cost. Thus each adjacent pair of horizontal moves in the sequence (i.e., consecutive E/E) corresponds to a straight pass through a V block and costs c. Similarly, each adjacent pair of vertical moves (N/N or S/S) corresponds to a straight pass through an H block and costs c.

Therefore, if the monotone path is represented as a sequence of moves with H moves and V moves, the number of straight constrained passes is the number of adjacent equal-direction moves? More precisely, adjacent moves of the same axis (both horizontal or both vertical) and same sign (which is forced by monotonicity) create a straight pass through the intermediate block of the opposite orientation? Let's check:
- Two horizontal moves: H-V-H. Middle V constrained horizontally. Yes.
- Two vertical moves: V-H-V. Middle H constrained vertically. Yes.
So every pair of consecutive moves along the same axis adds c. If moves alternate axes (turns), no straight pass cost.

Thus for a monotone path with D moves, the number of same-axis adjacent pairs is D-1 minus number of turns. If we choose the order of H and V moves, we can minimize turns? Thus our earlier "move direction" alone not enough; need know orientation of source/target. But along a path, orientation alternates, and the constrained block is:
- Horizontal move: V block. If move E, source is H, target V -> V is target, crossed left side -> low. If move W, source V, target H -> V is source, crossed right side -> high. Since along a path orientations alternate, a horizontal move can be either H->V or V->H depending on the parity of the starting block and position in path. Similarly vertical move N:
- H->V N: H source top high.
- V->H N: H target bottom low.

- H->V S: H source bottom low.

Thus the value for a move depends on the parity of the block index along the path. Since orientation alternates every move, the sequence of constrained values for moves of the same axis alternates? Let's derive.

Let's define a path as sequence of blocks B_0, B_1, ..., B_D. Orientation O_m = parity of B_m. Moves alternate orientation. For each move m (from B_m to B_{m+1}), the constrained block is:
- If move is horizontal (E/W): constrained block is the V block among B_m, B_{m+1}. The value is low if the move crosses the V block's left side, high if right side.
  * If B_m is H, B_{m+1} is V (move E or W? Actually if moving E, B_{m+1} is to the right; if B_m H and B_{m+1} V, then move E crosses V left side -> low. If moving W, B_m H, B_{m+1} V to the left; move W crosses V right side -> high.)
  * If B_m is V, B_{m+1} is H: moving E crosses V right side -> high; moving W crosses V left side -> low.
So for horizontal move, value = low if (B_m is H and dir=E) or (B_m is V and dir=W); high otherwise. Equivalently, value = low if the V block is entered from left? Let's use side of V: left side low, right side high. The side crossed is:
   - If V is target (move E from H to V): left side -> low.
   - If V is source (move W from V to H): left side -> low.
   - If V is target (move W from H to V): right side -> high.
   - If V is source (move E from V to H): right side -> high.
So value = low if the horizontal move is "towards the V block's left side", which happens when moving E with V target or W with V source. This is equivalent to the V block's coordinate relative to the move? Let's express in terms of the sign of Δi and the orientation of the V block.

Maybe we can avoid path-dependent values by considering the actual k constraints at blocks. The cost at straight passes is always adj(low,high) regardless of order (low->high or high->low), so straight pass cost independent of values. Endpoint adjustments depend on start_k vs first move's constrained value if first move constrains start, and end_k vs last move's constrained value if last move constrains end. Need compute those values for chosen first/last move.

Thus we need optimize over move order (sequence of A H-moves and B V-moves) to minimize:
- straight pass cost: c * (# same-axis adjacent pairs)
- start adjustment: if first move constrains start block, cost adj(start_k, val_first), else 0.
- end adjustment: if last move constrains end block, cost adj(val_last, end_k), else 0.

But note start adjustment and first move constraint: The first move constrains start block if the move is along start's constrained axis. Start orientation O0:
- If O0 = H, constrained axis y. So first move vertical (N/S) constrains start; horizontal first does not.
- If O0 = V, constrained axis x. So first move horizontal constrains start; vertical first does not.
Similarly, last move constrains end block if move is along end's constrained axis:
- If end orientation O_D = H, last move vertical constrains end; horizontal does not.
- If end orientation O_D = V, last move horizontal constrains end; vertical does not.

The value of the constraint on start/end depends on whether start/end is source or target in that move, and direction.

Let's compute start constraint value for each possible first move direction, given start orientation.

Start block B0. First move to B1. If move is along B0's constrained axis:
Case O0 = H (constrained vertical):
- First move N (B0 -> B1 above): B0 H source, crosses top side -> value high (b).
- First move S (B0 -> B1 below): B0 H source, crosses bottom side -> value low (0).
So value = high for N, low for S. This is simply sign of vertical move: + high, - low. (Because H is source; moving N/S from H always source.)
Case O0 = V (constrained horizontal):
- First move E: B0 V source, crosses right side -> value high.
- First move W: B0 V source, crosses left side -> value low.
So value = high for E, low for W. Again sign: + high, - low.
Thus start constraint value = high if first move is in positive direction along the constrained axis (N for H, E for V), low if negative (S for H, W for V). Good.

End constraint value for last move:
Case end orientation O_D = H (constrained vertical):
- Last move N (from B_{D-1} below to H above): H is target, crosses bottom side -> value low.
- Last move S (from B_{D-1} above to H below): H is target, crosses top side -> value high.
So value = low for N, high for S. Opposite of sign? For H target, N enters bottom low, S enters top high.
Case end orientation O_D = V (constrained horizontal):
- Last move E (from H left to V right): V target, crosses left side -> value low.
- Last move W (from H right to V left): V target, crosses right side -> value high.
So value = low for E, high for W. Opposite of sign.

Thus start constraint value = sign_positive ? high : low.
End constraint value = sign_positive ? low : high. (Because target side is opposite.)

Straight pass between move1 and move2? They are both E, middle block H(0,0). Wait two horizontal moves E,E: blocks V -> H -> V. The middle block is H, not V. Earlier I said two horizontal moves middle block is V? Let's check: Start V, move E to H, move E to V. Middle block H. Two horizontal moves through an H block. H block's constrained axis is vertical, so horizontal moves are free; no straight pass cost. Ah! My earlier statement "two horizontal moves -> middle V" assumed starting with H. In general, two consecutive horizontal moves have a middle block whose orientation depends on the orientation of the first block. If first block V, sequence V-H-V, middle H. If first block H, sequence H-V-H, middle V. The middle block is constrained by horizontal moves only if it is V. So straight pass cost occurs when the middle block's orientation is the one constrained by the axis of the moves. That is:
- Two horizontal moves: middle block is V if the first block of the pair is H; middle block is H if first block is V. Constrained by horizontal moves is V. So cost if first block is H? Let's verify sample path E,E,S from start V: first move V->H, second H->V. Pair moves 1-2: first block V, middle H. Horizontal moves through H are free, no cost. Pair moves? There are only two E's adjacent, yes no cost. So straight cost 0. Extra1 start adj. Good.

Thus straight pass cost depends on the orientation of the block between the two same-axis moves, which is determined by the parity of the move index. Since orientation alternates every move, the middle block orientation for moves m and m+1 is O_m? Wait two consecutive moves: B_m -> B_{m+1} -> B_{m+2}. The middle block is B_{m+1}. Its orientation is opposite to B_m. If the moves are horizontal, the constrained block for each horizontal move is the V block among the pair. The middle block B_{m+1} is constrained by both moves if B_{m+1} is V. If B_{m+1} is H, horizontal moves are free for it (but the V blocks at ends are constrained individually, not both on same block). So straight pass cost occurs only when the middle block is V for horizontal pair, or H for vertical pair. Equivalently, if two consecutive moves have the same axis, the middle block's orientation is:
- If the first move of the pair is from a block of orientation X to opposite. The middle block orientation is opposite of X.
- The axis of moves: horizontal constrains V, vertical constrains H.
So cost if middle block orientation equals the axis's constrained orientation:
  horizontal pair cost if middle block V.
  vertical pair cost if middle block H.
Since middle block orientation is opposite of the block before the pair. The block before the pair orientation is determined by start orientation and number of previous moves.

But maybe we can compute straight pass cost more simply by considering the sequence of moves and start orientation. The cost is c if (axis=Hmove and O_{m+1}=V) or (axis=Vmove and O_{m+1}=H). Since O_{m+1} is determined by parity of m+1 and O0. Let's encode orientation as 0 for H, 1 for V. O_{m+1} = O0 xor ((m+1) mod 2). Axis: horizontal maybe 0, vertical 1. Cost if axis == O_{m+1}? Let's check:
- horizontal axis constrains V (1). Cost if O_{m+1}=1. So cost if axis (H=0?) Let's define axis_constrained = 1 for horizontal (V), 0 for vertical (H)? If axis horizontal, constrained orientation V=1; if vertical, constrained H=0. So cost if O_{m+1} == (axis horizontal ? 1 : 0). Not simply axis==O.

But first, let's fully characterize using "move sequence" optimization.

We have A = |Δi|, B = |Δj|. Let h_sign = sign(Δi) (+1 for E, -1 for W). Let v_sign = sign(Δj) (+1 for N, -1 for S). If A=0 no horizontal moves; if B=0 no vertical moves.

Start orientation O0 = H if (i0+j0) even else V.
End orientation OD = H if (i0+h_sign*A + j0+v_sign*B) even? Since each move flips parity, OD = O0 xor (D mod 2). Also can compute.

The path is a sequence of A H-moves (direction h_sign) and B V-moves (direction v_sign). The actual directions are fixed signs; only order matters.

For each move, we need know if it constrains start/end if first/last.

Let's define for a move at position m (0-indexed from B_m to B_{m+1}), the constrained block is:
- If move is horizontal: the V block among B_m,B_{m+1}. Its orientation is V. The value (low/high) depends on whether the V block is source/target and direction sign:

  If O_m = V (source V):
    - move E (+): V source, crosses right side -> high.

    value = high if h_sign=+ else low.
  If O_m = H (target V):

    - move W (-): V target, crosses right side -> high.
    value = low if h_sign=+ else high.
So value = (O_m == V) ? (h_sign==+ ? high : low) : (h_sign==+ ? low : high).

Yes value = 1 XOR o XOR s. But maybe we don't need values except for endpoint adjustments and straight pass mismatch. Start constraint occurs if first move axis is along start's constrained axis:

So end constraint value = low if last move positive, high if negative.

Now, straight pass cost: For adjacent moves m and m+1 with same axis, the middle block B_{m+1} orientation O_{m+1}. The cost c if the middle block is the constrained orientation for that axis:
- horizontal axis: constrained orientation V (1). Cost if O_{m+1}=V.
- vertical axis: constrained orientation H (0). Cost if O_{m+1}=H.
But note if two moves same axis, the middle block is indeed constrained by both moves (arrival and departure) only if it is the constrained orientation for that axis. The two horizontal moves constrain V at first block (start) and V at third block (end), not middle. So no cost. Good.

Thus straight cost for each adjacent same-axis pair is c if O_{m+1} equals axis_constrained_orientation.

Can we express this in terms of move sequence and start orientation? Let's define axis of move m: a_m = 0 for horizontal, 1 for vertical. O_{m+1} = O0 xor ((m+1) mod 2). Axis_constrained orientation:
- if a_m = horizontal (0), constrained = V = 1.
- if a_m = vertical (1), constrained = H = 0.
So cost if O_{m+1} == (1 - a_m)? Since horizontal a=0 ->1, vertical a=1 ->0. That's O_{m+1} == 1 - a_m.

But a_m is same for both moves in pair. Let a be axis. Cost condition: O0 xor ((m+1) mod 2) == 1 - a.

This depends on m parity and O0. Maybe we can simplify by considering pairs of same-axis moves. Since a_m = a_{m+1}=a. Let p = (m+1) mod 2. Condition: O0 xor p == 1 - a. So for a given axis a, cost occurs when the pair starts at position m such that (m+1) mod 2 equals a fixed value C_a = (1 - a) xor O0.

- If O0=H (0):
  a=horizontal (0): C = 1 xor 0 = 1. So horizontal pair costs if m+1 odd => m even? Wait p=(m+1)%2=1 means m even. Example start H, sequence H->V->H horizontal pair (m=0): middle V, cost yes. m even. Good.
  a=vertical (1): C = 0 xor 0 = 0. Vertical pair costs if m+1 even => m odd. Example start H, after one move V, then vertical pair V->H->V? Middle H, cost yes at m=1. Good.

  a=horizontal (0): C = 1 xor 1 = 0. Horizontal pair costs if m+1 even => m odd. Example start V, horizontal pair at m=1? Sequence V->H->V (m=0? Wait start V, moves: m=0 V->H, m=1 H->V. Pair m=0,1 horizontal, middle H, no cost. Condition m=0, m+1=1 odd, C=0 -> no cost. Good. If pair starts at m=1? Need two horizontal moves at m=1,2: sequence ? V->H (m0), H->V (m1), V->H (m2)?   a=vertical (1): C = 0 xor 1 = 1. Vertical pair costs if m+1 odd => m even. Example start V, vertical pair m=0? V->H (m0), H->V (m1) vertical? Wait vertical moves at m=0 and m=1: V->H (vertical), H->V (vertical), middle H, cost yes. m=0, m+1=1 odd, C=1 -> cost. Good.

So the cost of a same-axis pair depends on the parity of its starting position. This is important for optimizing move order: we want to place same-axis adjacent pairs at positions where they don't cost, if possible, or minimize number of costly pairs.

But maybe we can choose start orientation? No fixed. Need compute minimal number of costly same-axis adjacent pairs in a sequence with A horizontal and B vertical moves, where cost for a same-axis pair at start index m is:
- horizontal pair cost if m parity = P_H = ? Let's derive in terms of m parity (0-based start index).
Condition for horizontal pair cost: O0 xor ((m+1)%2) == 1.
Let O0 bit. If O0=0 (H): cost if (m+1)%2=1 -> m even.
If O0=1 (V): cost if (m+1)%2=0 -> m odd.
So horizontal pair cost if m%2 == (O0? 1 : 0)? Let's check:
O0=0: m even (0) -> cost. So cost if m%2=0.
O0=1: m odd -> cost. So cost if m%2=1.
Thus horizontal pair cost if m%2 == O0.
Vertical pair cost condition: O0 xor ((m+1)%2) == 0.
If O0=0: cost if (m+1)%2=0 -> m odd. So cost if m%2=1.
If O0=1: cost if (m+1)%2=1 -> m even. So cost if m%2=0.
Thus vertical pair cost if m%2 != O0? Actually O0=0 -> m odd (1), O0=1 -> m even (0). So vertical pair cost if m%2 == 1-O0.

So:
- Horizontal same-axis pair at start index m costs if m parity = O0.
- Vertical same-axis pair at start index m costs if m parity = 1-O0.

Interesting: For a given start orientation, one axis's pairs cost on even positions, the other on odd positions.

Now, we need choose a sequence of A H-moves and B V-moves. The total number of adjacent same-axis pairs is:
- Among H-moves: if all H moves are grouped in runs, each run of length L contributes L-1 horizontal pairs. Total horizontal pairs = A - (# horizontal runs).
- Similarly vertical pairs = B - (# vertical runs).
The start indices of these pairs are determined by the run positions.

We want minimize weighted cost: each horizontal pair costs c if its start index parity = O0; each vertical pair costs c if its start index parity = 1-O0. Pairs of the other parity cost 0.

This is a combinatorial optimization. Since c is constant, we can minimize number of costly pairs.

Let's analyze the general problem more systematically.

We have a path as a sequence of moves. We can choose the order of A horizontal and B vertical moves. The cost components:

1. Start adjustment cost S_adj:
   - If A>0 and first move is horizontal and O0=V, or if B>0 and first move is vertical and O0=H. More simply, if first move axis == constrained_axis_start (vertical for H, horizontal for V), then cost = adj(start_k, start_val), where start_val = high if first move positive, low if negative. Else 0.
   Let's denote start_axis_constrained = 'V' if O0=H else 'H'. If the first move is of that axis, cost depends on sign. If no moves of that axis? Then first move is other axis, no start constraint. If A=0 or B=0, first move axis is the only axis; if that axis is constrained, cost applies.

2. End adjustment cost E_adj:
   - If last move axis == constrained_axis_end (vertical for H, horizontal for V), cost = adj(end_val, end_k), where end_val = low if last move positive, high if negative. Else 0.
   constrained_axis_end = 'V' if OD=H else 'H'.

3. Straight pass cost:
   - For each adjacent pair of moves with same axis, cost c if the pair's start index parity is "bad" for that axis as above. Else 0.

We need minimize over all sequences with A H-moves and B V-moves.

This is a finite-state DP over counts and parity? But A,B up to 1e16, need closed form. However the cost structure is simple: only adjacent same-axis pairs at certain parities cost. We can think of arranging runs of H and V moves. The sequence is an alternating sequence of runs: e.g., H^a1 V^b1 H^a2 V^b2 ... with positive run lengths. Number of horizontal runs r_H, vertical runs r_V. They differ by at most 1. The total horizontal pairs = A - r_H, vertical pairs = B - r_V. Each run of length L contributes L-1 pairs, all with start indices consecutive within the run. The parity of the first pair in a run depends on the starting position of the run. Within a run, pairs at consecutive indices have alternating parity. So for a run of length L, the number of costly pairs is the number of indices m in [run_start, run_start+L-2] with m%2 == bad_parity_axis.

Thus the cost of a run depends on its starting position and length. We can choose run lengths and order to minimize total costly pairs plus endpoint adjustments.

Since c is constant, maybe we can compute minimal total extra by considering the sequence as a path in a 2-state automaton and using formulas for counts. But A,B huge; however the number of runs is at most 2*min(A,B)+1, which can be huge if min huge. But we can derive minimal costly pairs in terms of A,B,O0.

Let's first solve straight pass cost minimization alone (ignoring endpoint adjustments), then incorporate endpoints by choosing first/last move.

Because endpoint adjustments only depend on the first and last move axes and signs (signs fixed by Δ), not on internal order except first/last. We can enumerate possible first/last axes (horizontal/vertical) and compute minimal straight cost subject to those endpoints, then add endpoint adj. There are at most 4 combinations (first axis, last axis) feasible if counts allow. Then take min.

So key subproblem: Given A horizontal moves, B vertical moves, start orientation O0, and specified first axis f (H/V) and last axis l (H/V) (if A/B counts allow), find minimal number of costly same-axis adjacent pairs.

This is like arranging a binary sequence with A zeros (H-moves) and B ones (V-moves), with given first and last symbols, minimizing a cost on adjacent equal pairs depending on position parity and symbol.

Let's formalize.

Let symbol 0 = horizontal move, 1 = vertical move.
Bad parity for symbol 0 pairs: p0 = O0 (since horizontal pair cost if m%2 == O0).
Bad parity for symbol 1 pairs: p1 = 1 - O0.

Cost for an adjacent equal pair of symbol s at start index m (0-based) is 1 if m%2 == p_s, else 0. (Multiply by c later.)

We need minimize total cost over binary sequences with A zeros, B ones, first=f, last=l.

This is a 1D sequence with position-dependent costs on equal adjacencies. Since costs only depend on parity of position, we can think of positions alternate parity. The cost of a run of symbol s of length L starting at position r:
- It has L-1 internal pairs at start indices r, r+1, ..., r+L-2.
- Number of indices with parity p_s in that range.
If L=1, no pairs.
If L>=2, count = floor? Let's compute count of integers m in [r, r+L-2] with m%2 = p.
Let n = L-1 (number of pairs). The first parity is r%2. Among n consecutive parities, count = n//2 if first parity != p? Actually if n even, exactly n/2 of each. If n odd, one more of the starting parity. So count = n//2 + (1 if (n odd and r%2==p) else 0). Equivalently count = (n + (1 if r%2==p else 0)) // 2? Let's test n=3, r parity 0, p=0: parities 0,1,0 -> count2. (3+1)//2=2. p=1: count1. (3+0)//2=1. Good. So count = (n + (r%2==p)) // 2.

Thus cost of a run = c * ((L-1 + indicator(r parity == p_s)) // 2).

This is nonlinear but manageable.

We can choose run lengths. The sequence of runs alternates symbols. If first and last symbols specified, the number of runs is determined:
- If first == last, number of runs is odd: 2R+1, where R = min? Specifically if A and B counts. The symbols with larger count may have more runs.
- If first != last, number of runs is even: 2R.

Actually the number of runs of each symbol:
Let r0 = number of runs of symbol 0, r1 = number of runs of symbol 1.
If sequence starts with 0 and ends with 0: r0 = r1+1.
If starts 1 ends 1: r1 = r0+1.
If starts 0 ends 1 or starts 1 ends 0: r0 = r1.
Also r0 <= A, r1 <= B, and positive if count>0.

Given A,B, the possible r0,r1 are constrained:
- If A=0, r0=0; if B=0, r1=0.
- Generally, for a binary sequence with counts A,B, the number of runs of each symbol can vary between 1 and count, with |r0-r1| <=1 and if both counts >0, both r0,r1 >=1. Specifically:
  If first=last=s, then r_s = r_other + 1.
  If first!=last, r0=r1.

We can choose run lengths positive integers summing to counts. The cost depends on run lengths and starting positions.

This is reminiscent of distributing "extra" moves within runs. To minimize cost, we might want runs as short as possible (more runs) to avoid same-axis pairs, but run count limited by counts and endpoints. The minimal number of same-axis pairs total is max(0, |A-B|-1) if no parity cost? Actually total same-axis pairs = (A-r0)+(B-r1). To minimize total pairs, maximize r0+r1. Max runs = 2*min(A,B) + (1 if A!=B? depending endpoints). The minimal total pairs is max(0, |A-B|-1) as before. But costly pairs may be less than total pairs because some pairs at good parity cost 0. So we might not need to minimize total pairs; we can place pairs at good parities.

Since cost per pair is either 0 or 1 (times c), and good parity pairs are free, we can have many same-axis pairs at no cost if they occur at the right parity. Thus the optimal may use long runs starting at good parity to get free pairs, reducing number of runs.

Let's analyze run cost more directly. For a run of symbol s of length L starting at position r:
- If L=1: cost0.
- If L>=2:
  If r%2 == p_s, then the first pair is costly, then parities alternate, so costly pairs count = ceil((L-1)/2)? Let's compute: n=L-1. If first parity p, count = (n+1)//2 = ceil(n/2). Because among n pairs starting with bad parity, bad count = ceil(n/2). If r%2 != p_s, count = floor(n/2).
So a run starting at the bad parity has roughly half its internal pairs costly; starting at good parity has roughly half (floor). The difference is at most 1.

Thus each run contributes about (L-1)/2 costly pairs. Total costly pairs roughly (total same-axis pairs)/2, but parity can adjust.

Maybe there is a simpler invariant: The cost of a same-axis pair at index m is 1 if O_{m+1} == constrained orientation. This is equivalent to the middle block being "bad". Perhaps the number of bad straight passes equals the number of times the path goes straight through a block whose orientation is the constrained one. Could be related to the parity of the block coordinates at turns.

Let's try another approach: Instead of optimizing sequences, maybe we can compute distance directly using a BFS on a coarse graph of "k categories" with block coordinates. Since extra cost small, we can derive by considering the shortest path in a graph where each block move costs 1 and k adjustment costs 0/1/2. Thus at each adjacent pair position m, there is a preferred symbol e_m (alternating with m). If the two moves are equal to each other and equal to e_m, the pair costs. If the two moves are equal to the other symbol, it's free. If the two moves differ, no pair.

So the cost is incurred only on runs of the symbol that matches the alternating pattern e_m. Runs of the opposite symbol are free.

This is very helpful. e_m alternates: e_0 = O0, e_1 = 1-O0, e_2 = O0, ...

So at even positions, preferred symbol = O0; at odd positions, preferred = 1-O0.

   e_{m+1}=1-e_m.

- y_m = y_{m+1}: turn (no cost).
- y_m=0, y_{m+1}=1: same symbol, costly.
- y_m=1, y_{m+1}=0: same symbol, free.

We have variables E_t, O_t in {0,1}. Counts:

b = sum O_t (over all O positions)
C = a-b.

We want minimize sum A_t + sum B_t.

This is like a path on pairs. Maybe we can solve by considering the number of 1s on E and O.

Thus the problem reduces to: Given required difference C between ones on even and odd positions, what is the minimum number of 0->1 transitions in a binary sequence?

This is a known problem. Let's solve it.

Let D, E=ceil(D/2), Od=floor(D/2). We need choose a,b with a-b=C, 0<=a<=E, 0<=b<=Od. Equivalently, a = b+C. b can range:
max(0, -C) <= b <= min(Od, E-C).
So feasible iff max(0,-C) <= min(Od, E-C).

We want minimize inc = #0->1 transitions.

We can think of constructing y to minimize inc. Since inc counts starts of 1-runs (except leading). To minimize, we want as few 1-runs as possible, ideally one run (possibly leading). A sequence with one 1-run has form 0^p 1^q 0^r (if not leading) or 1^q 0^r (leading) or 0^p 1^q (trailing). Let's compute a-b for such forms.

A single 1-run of length q starting at position st, ending at st+q-1.
- If st=0 (leading run): positions 0..q-1 are 1. a = ones on even among first q = ceil(q/2), b = floor(q/2). C = a-b = q mod 2 (0 if q even, 1 if q odd). So C is 0 or1.
- If st>0 and st+q = D (trailing run): positions st..D-1 are 1. The number of ones on even/odd depends on st and q. Let's compute C = a-b. Since total positions D, zeros prefix length st. The ones suffix. a = E - (# zeros on even). b = Od - (# zeros on odd). Zeros prefix length st has even count ceil(st/2), odd floor(st/2). So a = E - ceil(st/2), b = Od - floor(st/2). C = (E-Od) - (ceil(st/2)-floor(st/2)) = (D mod 2) - (st mod 2). Since E-Od = D mod 2. So C can be -1,0,1? st mod2 0/1, Dmod2 0/1. If D even, C = - (st mod2): 0 or -1. If D odd, C = 1 - (st mod2): 1 or0. So C in {-1,0,1}.
- If 1-run is internal (0^p 1^q 0^r with p>0,r>0): one 0->1 at start of run, one 1->0 at end. C? The ones are in a contiguous block not touching ends. Let's compute. Positions of ones from st to en. a-b for a contiguous block of length q starting at st. There is a formula: a-b = (number of even positions in block) - (number of odd positions in block). For a contiguous block of length q starting at parity st%2:
   If q even, equal even/odd -> C=0.
   If q odd, the starting parity has one more -> C = 1 if st even, -1 if st odd.
So C ∈ {-1,0,1}. Thus any single 1-run gives C ∈ {-1,0,1}. If required C outside {-1,0,1}, we need multiple 1-runs. Each additional 1-run can change C by? A 1-run contributes either -1,0,1 depending on its start parity and length parity. The total C is sum of contributions of all 1-runs (since a-b additive over runs). Each run contribution is -1,0,1. The number of 0->1 transitions equals number of 1-runs that are not at the very beginning (i.e., preceded by 0). If the first run starts at position 0, it doesn't add a 0->1; otherwise each run adds 1. So inc = (#runs) - (1 if first run starts at 0 else 0).

We need achieve a given C with minimum inc. Since each run contribution is at most 1 in absolute value, to get |C| large we need at least |C| runs? But a run can contribute 1 or -1. provided feasible. But C can be large; this gives O(1).

Let's express C in terms of A,B,O0:

We can simplify:

C = ((B - A + 1) // 2) if O0 == 0 else ((A - B + 1) // 2).

else: -C

But wait, this straight cost_pairs is the number of costly same-axis pairs, each costing c. So straight_extra = c * cost_pairs.

Now, does this straight cost minimization already account for the ability to choose first/last moves? The y sequence optimization did not constrain first/last move axes except through the counts? Actually any y sequence yields a move sequence s = y xor e. The first move s_0 = y_0 xor e_0 = y_0 xor O0. The last move s_{D-1} = y_{D-1} xor e_{D-1}. So first/last axes are determined by y. The y optimization for minimal inc may produce some first/last axes. Endpoint adjustments depend on first/last axes. We cannot independently choose endpoint axes; they are tied to y. However, we can choose among y sequences achieving minimal inc; maybe different y with same inc have different first/last axes. We need incorporate endpoint costs.

Our straight cost minimization gave a lower bound and construction for y. But endpoint costs may force a different y with slightly higher inc to reduce endpoint adjustments. Since endpoint adjustments are at most 2 (or 1 for K=2) and inc cost per pair is c (1 or 2), we need optimize total.

Maybe we can extend the y framework to include endpoint costs. The total extra cost = c * inc + S_adj + E_adj, where S_adj/E_adj depend on first/last move axes and signs. We can compute S_adj and E_adj as functions of y_0 and y_{D-1} perhaps.

Let's express first move axis s_0:
s_0 = y_0 xor O0. If s_0=0 horizontal, s_0=1 vertical.
Start constraint occurs if first move axis == start_constrained_axis:
- O0=H (0): constrained axis vertical (s=1). So start adj applies if s_0=1.
- O0=V (1): constrained axis horizontal (s=0). So start adj applies if s_0=0.
Equivalently, start adj applies if s_0 == 1 - O0? Since O0=0 -> s=1; O0=1 -> s=0. Yes s_0 = 1 - O0.
But s_0 = y_0 xor O0. So condition y_0 xor O0 = 1 - O0. This implies y_0 = 1. Let's check:
If O0=0: s0=1 -> y0=1. If O0=1: s0=0 -> y0=1. Yes! Start constraint applies iff y_0 = 1. Interesting.
Start value:
- If O0=H and s0=1 (vertical), sign v_sign. Start value = high if v_sign=+ (N), low if - (S).
- If O0=V and s0=0 (horizontal), sign h_sign. Start value = high if h_sign=+ (E), low if - (W).
Can we express in terms of y_0? y_0=1 always when constraint applies. The sign is the direction of the first move, which is h_sign if s0=0, v_sign if s0=1. Let's define sign_first = h_sign if s0=0 else v_sign. Start value = high if sign_first=+ else low. So S_adj = adj(start_k, high if sign_first=+ else low) if y0=1 else 0.

Similarly, last move axis s_last = y_{D-1} xor e_{D-1}. End constraint applies if last move axis == end_constrained_axis.
End orientation OD = O0 xor (D mod 2). End constrained axis:
- If OD=H (0): vertical (s=1).
- If OD=V (1): horizontal (s=0).
So end adj applies if s_last = 1 - OD.
Compute e_last = O0 xor ((D-1) mod 2). Let's find condition in terms of y_last.
s_last = y_last xor e_last. We want s_last = 1 - OD.
OD = O0 xor (D mod 2).
e_last = O0 xor ((D-1) mod 2) = O0 xor (1 - (D mod 2))? Since (D-1) mod2 = 1 - (D mod2). So e_last = O0 xor (1 - p) where p=D mod2.
Let's test condition y_last = ? 
Compute s_last = y_last xor O0 xor (1-p). Want = 1 - (O0 xor p).
So y_last = (1 - (O0 xor p)) xor O0 xor (1-p).
Let's simplify. Let p = D%2.
Case O0=0:
  OD = p. End constrained axis = 1-p (vertical if p=0? Wait OD=H if p=0, constrained vertical s=1; if p=1, OD=V constrained horizontal s=0. So s_last = 1-p).
  e_last = 0 xor (1-p) = 1-p.
  s_last = y_last xor (1-p). Want = 1-p => y_last=0.
Case O0=1:
  OD = 1 xor p. End constrained axis = 1 - OD = p.
  e_last = 1 xor (1-p) = p.
  s_last = y_last xor p. Want = p => y_last=0.
So end constraint applies iff y_last = 0! Nice.

End value:
- If OD=H, s_last=1 (vertical), sign v_sign. End value = low if v_sign=+ (N), high if - (S) (target H).
- If OD=V, s_last=0 (horizontal), sign h_sign. End value = low if h_sign=+ (E), high if - (W).
So E_adj = adj(end_val, end_k) if y_last=0 else 0, where end_val = low if sign_last=+ else high.

Thus endpoint adjustments depend only on y_0 and y_last:
- If y_0=1, pay start adj based on first move sign.
- If y_last=0, pay end adj based on last move sign.
If y_0=0, first move is the unconstrained axis, no start adj.
If y_last=1, last move is unconstrained axis, no end adj.

Great! This is very useful.

Now, the y sequence optimization with endpoint costs: We need minimize c * inc + S_adj(y0) + E_adj(ylast) subject to a-b=C.

The signs h_sign, v_sign are fixed by Δi, Δj. The first move sign is h_sign if s0=0 else v_sign. But s0 = y0 xor O0. If y0=1, s0 = 1 xor O0. Let's compute sign_first when y0=1:
- If O0=0: s0=1 -> vertical, sign = v_sign.
- If O0=1: s0=0 -> horizontal, sign = h_sign.
So start_val = high if (O0==0 ? v_sign : h_sign) == + else low.
Similarly, if y_last=0, s_last = e_last = O0 xor ((D-1)%2). But we can compute sign_last:
- If OD=H (0): s_last=1 -> vertical, sign = v_sign.
- If OD=V (1): s_last=0 -> horizontal, sign = h_sign.
End_val = low if sign_last=+ else high.
So endpoint costs are constants depending on O0, OD, signs, and start/end k, applied when y0=1 / ylast=0.

Thus total extra = c * inc + (S if y0=1 else 0) + (E if ylast=0 else 0).

We need minimize over binary y sequences with a-b=C.

Now, inc = #0->1 transitions. We have a formula for minimal inc without endpoint constraints. We also need consider y0 and ylast. We can enumerate possible values of y0 and ylast (4 combinations) and find minimal inc for each, then add endpoint costs. Since inc formula might be extended with boundary conditions.

So subproblem: Given D, E, Od, C, and specified y0 = u, ylast = v, find minimal number of 0->1 transitions in y.

This is a classic DP with boundary constraints. We can derive formula similar to before.

Let's solve this.

We have a binary sequence y[0..D-1]. Counts difference a-b=C. We want minimize inc = # indices m=0..D-2 with y_m=0,y_{m+1}=1.

Boundary y0=u, ylast=v.

We can think in terms of runs of 1s. inc = number of 1-runs not starting at position 0. If y0=1, the first run (if any) starts at 0 and is free; if y0=0, every 1-run costs 1.

Let r = number of 1-runs. Let lead = 1 if y0=1 else 0. Then inc = r - lead. (If y0=1, there is at least one 1-run; if y0=0, lead=0.)

We need achieve C = sum contributions of 1-runs. Each 1-run contribution is:
- For a run starting at even position: contribution = 1 if length odd, 0 if length even.
- Starting at odd position: contribution = -1 if length odd, 0 if length even.
(Since a-b for a contiguous block: if length even, equal; if odd, +1 for even start, -1 for odd start.)

Also, the runs are separated by at least one 0. The positions of runs and lengths determine C and also ylast (whether last run ends at D-1). But maybe we can ignore exact lengths except parity and start parity, as long as feasible.

We want minimize r - lead for given C and boundary ylast.

Since each run contributes at most 1 in absolute value (0, ±1). To get C, we need a multiset of run contributions summing to C. The number of runs r is at least |C| (if using ±1 contributions) but can be more with 0 contributions. Since inc = r - lead, extra 0-contribution runs increase inc (unless lead? they still increase r), so we should use no 0-contribution runs unless needed for boundary/feasibility. Thus minimal r for given C is |C|, except C=0 can have r=0 (all zeros) or r=2? Actually C=0 can be achieved with r=0 (no ones) inc0 if boundaries allow. If y0=1 or ylast=1, need at least one run, which must have contribution 0 (even length) to keep C=0. So r may be 1 with contribution0. That's okay.

Boundary ylast=v affects whether the last run ends at D-1. If v=1, the last run must end at D-1. If v=0, the last run ends before D-1 (or no run). This may affect the parity of run start? The contribution of a run depends only on start parity and length parity, not on end position, except that if it ends at D-1, its length parity is determined by start and D-1. But we can choose start parity and length parity independently? A run starts at some position; its start parity can be even or odd. Its length parity can be chosen by ending position, as long as there is room. Boundary v=1 forces the final run to have end = D-1, so length parity = (D-1 - start) mod2. This links contribution to start parity and D parity. But we can choose start parity.

Maybe easier: Use the inc-dec relation with boundaries.

Recall inc - dec = ylast - y0. dec = #1->0 transitions. inc = dec + v - u.
Also, total variation? We can express dec in terms of runs: dec = number of 1-runs that end before D-1 (i.e., not touching end). If ylast=1, the last run touches end, so dec = r - 1? Actually each 1-run except the last (if it ends at D-1) has a 1->0 transition at its end. If ylast=0, every run ends before D-1, so dec = r. If ylast=1, dec = r-1 (assuming r>0; if r=0 then ylast=0). Thus inc = r - lead (as before) and dec = r - v? Let's check: If v=1, dec = r-1 = r - v. If v=0, dec = r = r - v. So dec = r - v. Then inc = dec + v - u = r - u. Wait inc = r - lead, and lead = u. Yes inc = r - u. Good.

So inc = r - u, where r is number of 1-runs. This matches.

Now, C = sum contributions of runs. Each run contribution is in {-1,0,1}. Let r_plus, r_minus, r_zero counts, with r = r_plus+r_minus+r_zero, C = r_plus - r_minus.

We want minimize inc = r - u = r_plus+r_minus+r_zero - u, subject to:
- r_plus - r_minus = C.
- Feasibility of run starts/ends with boundary v.
- r_plus,r_minus,r_zero nonnegative integers.

To minimize r for given C, set r_zero=0 and r_minus=0 if C>=0, so r_plus=C, r_minus=0, r=C. If C<0, r_minus=-C, r_plus=0, r=-C. Then inc = |C| - u. But earlier unconstrained minimal inc for C>0 was C-1, not C-u with u? In unconstrained, we could choose u=1 (y0=1) to get inc=C-1. If u=0, inc=C. So the formula inc = |C| - u gives C-1 for C>0,u=1; for C<0,u=0 gives -C; for C<0,u=1 gives -C-1, which might be negative? But inc cannot be negative. If C<0 and u=1, can inc be -C-1? Example C=-1,u=1: formula gives0. Is that possible? y starts with 1, one run contribution -1. A run starting at odd position with odd length contributes -1. But if y starts with 1, the first run starts at even position 0, contribution +1 (if odd) or 0 (if even), cannot be -1. So a negative contribution run cannot be the leading run starting at 0. Thus r_minus runs cannot be at position 0. Therefore if u=1, the leading run is at position 0 and has contribution 0 or +1, not -1. So for C<0, we need at least one negative run in addition to the leading run? Let's analyze.

The leading run (if u=1) starts at even position 0, so its contribution is either 0 (even length) or +1 (odd length). It cannot be -1. Therefore:
- If C > 0, we can use the leading run as +1, plus additional +1 runs if C>1. inc = r -1 = C -1 (if r=C). Good.
- If C = 0, leading run contribution must be 0, r=1, inc=0. Good.
- If C < 0, the leading run contributes 0 or +1, which makes C even more positive; to get negative total, we need negative runs. The leading run is "wasted" for negative C; it increases r without helping. Thus we should set u=0 to avoid a wasted leading run. If u=1 forced, minimal inc = ? We need r_plus - r_minus = C <0. Let leading run contribution c0 ∈ {0,1}. Other runs contributions sum to C - c0. To minimize r, choose c0=0 (leading even length) so other runs need sum C. Then r = 1 + (-C) (using -C negative runs) = 1 - C. inc = r - u = (1 - C) -1 = -C. If c0=1, need C-1 negative, r=1 + (1-C) = 2-C, inc=1-C, worse. So minimal inc for C<0,u=1 is -C. Same as u=0? For u=0, inc = -C. So negative C cost -C regardless of u? Let's test with B=0 O=0 D=3 C=-1, y=0,1,0 has u=0 inc1. If u=1 forced, can we have y=1,0,1? C=0? Not -1. y=1,1,0? C=1? Thus for C<0, minimal inc = -C regardless of u (if feasible). For C>=0, minimal inc = max(0, C - u)? Let's check:
C=0: if u=0, r=0 inc0; if u=1, leading run contribution0 r=1 inc0. So inc=0 = max(0,0-u)=0.
C>0: if u=1, inc=C-1; if u=0, inc=C. So inc = C - u. Since u∈{0,1}, C-u. For C=1,u=1 inc0; u=0 inc1. Good.
So minimal inc for given u (and no ylast constraint?) is:
inc_min(u) = 
  if C >= 0: C - u
  else: -C
provided feasible. This matches earlier unconstrained by choosing u=1 if C>0 (inc C-1), u=0 if C<=0 (inc -C).

Now, we also have boundary ylast = v. Does it affect minimal inc? It may require the last run to end at D-1 if v=1, or not if v=0. We can often adjust run lengths to satisfy v without changing r or contributions, by adding a zero-contribution run? Need check.

Given a run configuration with r runs and contributions summing to C, can we arrange it to end with v while preserving r and inc? The last run's end position determines v. If v=1, the last run must touch D-1. If v=0, the last run must not touch D-1 (or no run). We can choose the length of the last run to make it end at D-1 or not, as long as there is space. - If C > 0: inc = C - u? But if v=1 and u=0, can we have C positive runs with inc=C and last run touching end? Example C=1,u=0,v=1,D=3. Need one +1 run not at start, ending at last. A run starting at even position (2?) length1 at pos2 gives +1, y=0,0,1, inc1 (0->1 at edge1), v=1. inc=C=1. Good.

Let's derive a general formula for inc_min(u,v) using run counts and boundary.

We have inc = r - u. We want minimize r subject to:
- r_plus - r_minus = C.
- r = r_plus+r_minus+r_zero.
- Boundary u: if u=1, there is a leading run at position0. If u=0, no run at position0.
- Boundary v: if v=1, the last run ends at D-1. If v=0, the last run does not end at D-1 (or no run).

The contribution of a run depends on start parity and length parity. But maybe any multiset of run contributions with total C and r runs can be arranged with given boundaries if there is enough length. Since D can be large (A,B up to 1e16), but if D small, feasibility matters. We can handle feasibility separately.

Let's first assume D large enough. Then:
- If C > 0:
  Need r_plus - r_minus = C. To minimize r, set r_minus=0, r_zero=0, r_plus=C. So r=C.
  inc = C - u.
  Boundary v: Can arrange C positive runs (+1) with first run at 0 if u=1, and last run ending at D-1 if v=1. Positive runs must start at even positions and have odd length. We can place them at even positions separated by zeros. If v=1, the last positive run can be extended to end at D-1 (odd length if start even and D-1 parity? Need start even, length = D-1 - start +1 = D-start. For length odd, need D-start odd => start parity = 1-D mod2? Wait length = end-start+1. If end=D-1, length parity = (D-1 - start +1) mod2 = (D-start) mod2. For contribution +1, length must be odd, so D-start odd => start parity = 1 - D mod2? Let's compute: D-start odd => start parity = 1 - D mod2. If D even, start odd; but positive run must start even. Contradiction! Let's check example D=5 (odd), positive run touching end at pos2 length3 (start even, D=5 odd, D-start=3 odd yes start parity even = 1-D mod2? 1-1=0 even). For D even, say D=4, positive run touching end must start odd? Example D=4, v=1, need +1 run at end. Positions 0,1,2,3. A run starting at1 length3 (1,2,3) start odd length odd -> contribution -1, not +1. Starting at2 length2 even contribution0. Starting at0 length4 even contribution0. So a +1 run cannot touch the end when D is even! Because to have odd length ending at odd index D-1, start must be even? Wait D=4, end=3 odd, length odd => start even? 3-0+1=4 even; 3-1+1=3 odd start odd. So start parity = end parity for odd length? length = end-start+1 odd => end-start even => start parity = end parity. End parity = (D-1)%2. For D even, end odd, so start odd. Positive +1 requires start even. Thus impossible. Therefore, if v=1 and D even, the last run cannot be a +1 run. It must be a 0 or -1 contribution run. This may increase r.

Ah! Boundary v imposes parity constraints on the last run's contribution. Similarly, leading run at position0: length odd => contribution +1; length even => 0. So leading run can be +1 or 0, not -1, consistent.

Thus we need account for D parity and v.

Let's analyze run contribution and end boundary.

A run is a maximal block of 1s. Its contribution c_run = 
- 0 if length even.
- +1 if length odd and start even.
- -1 if length odd and start odd.

If the run ends at D-1 (v=1), then length parity = (D-1 - start +1) = D - start mod2. So:
- If D even: length parity = start parity (since D-start ≡ start mod2). Thus contribution = +1 if start even and length odd? Wait length odd iff start parity = D mod2? D even => D-start even? Let's compute: D even, D-start parity = start parity. Length odd iff start parity =1. So if start odd, length odd -> contribution -1. If start even, length even -> contribution 0. So a run touching end when D even can contribute -1 (start odd, odd length) or 0 (start even, even length), but not +1.
- If D odd: length parity = 1 - start parity. Length odd iff start parity =0. So if start even, contribution +1; if start odd, length even contribution0. Thus a run touching end when D odd can contribute +1 (start even) or 0 (start odd), but not -1.

Similarly, leading run at position0:
- If length odd: contribution +1 (start even).
- If length even: contribution 0.
So leading run can be +1 or 0, not -1, regardless of D.

Thus boundary v=1 restricts the last run's contribution to:
- D even: possible contributions -1 or 0.
- D odd: possible contributions +1 or 0.

Boundary u=1 restricts first run contribution to +1 or 0.

Now, if C>0 and v=1 with D even, the last run cannot be +1. But if r=C and all runs are +1, impossible. We may need to replace one +1 run with a 0 or -1 run and add extra +1 runs to keep C, increasing r. Let's compute minimal r with boundary constraints.

This is getting detailed but manageable. We can derive minimal inc for each (u,v) using a small case analysis on C sign and D parity.

Alternatively, since D = A+B can be huge but we only need formula. Let's enumerate possible scenarios.

We have C = a-b. Feasibility also requires |C| <= E or Od etc. But if C is large, D large enough.

Let's define possible run contribution types:
- P: +1 (start even, odd length)
- N: -1 (start odd, odd length)
- Z: 0 (even length, any start? even length gives 0 regardless of start parity; but start parity matters for boundary? For a run not touching boundaries, even length contribution0, start can be even or odd. For leading run at 0, even length Z. So Z possible at trailing always? D even start even; D odd start odd, yes always possible.)
- Leading run at 0 can be P (odd length) or Z (even length).

We need a multiset of runs with total contribution C, with first run type constrained by u, last run type constrained by v, and runs arranged in sequence with alternating? Runs are separated by zeros. Any sequence of run types is feasible if there is enough space; types determine start parity and length parity, but we can choose lengths to fit. Since D huge, likely feasible except small D. We'll assume feasibility and later check with constraints.

We want minimize r = number of runs, inc = r - u.

Given total C, each run contributes at most 1 in absolute value. The minimal number of runs ignoring boundary is |C| (using all P if C>0, all N if C<0, or 0 runs if C=0). Boundary constraints may force some runs of "wrong" type, requiring extra runs to compensate.

Let's handle by cases for C sign.

Case C > 0:
We need total positive C. Runs can be P (+1), Z (0), N (-1). To minimize r, we prefer P runs. Let p = number of P, n = number of N, z = number of Z. C = p - n. r = p+n+z. Minimize r => n=0,z=0 if possible, so p=C, r=C. Boundary constraints:
- u=0: no leading constraint. v=0: no trailing constraint. Feasible if we can place C P runs not touching boundaries? P runs start even, odd length. They can be internal. Need at least? - u=0, v=1:
  Last run must be trailing. If D odd, trailing can be P. So all C runs P, last P, feasible. If D even, trailing cannot be P. Need adjust. Options:
    * Make last run Z (0) and add one more P to keep C: p=C+1, n=0, z=1, r=C+2? Wait C = p - n = C+1, need n=1 to balance? If last run Z contributes0, total C from other p P runs. Need p=C, z=1, r=C+1. But can we have a Z trailing run with D even? Yes start even even length. Then p=C P runs before it. Total r=C+1. inc = r - u = C+1 (if u=0).
